Yeah… irritable is probably the right word. It’s like I’ll be in this big, fired-up mood, talking about how our shop is about to take over solar work around here, and it all feels so obvious to me.

Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “serious” at first, because work is always messy, you know? Scheduling is a nightmare anyway, especially when one job runs long and then the supplier drops stuff at the wrong site, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault—

I can’t really say.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s been happening. For, I don’t know, months now I’ll sleep maybe three or four hours and then I’m just up before dawn like my brain’s already clocked in. I’ll start pricing materials or answering emails, or I’ll sketch out some wiring setup or system idea like it’s totally normal to be doing that at 4:30 in the morning.\n\nAnd the weird part is I don’t always feel wrecked from it. Like if I only slept that much after a long job, normally I’d be useless, but lately I’ll pop up feeling like I got a full night, at least at first. Then I get into the day and there’s all this stuff I’m trying to do, and—sorry, I’m getting off track—but yeah, much less sleep and still feeling pretty energized.\n\nDoctor: During these times when you sleep only three or four hours, do you also feel unusually cheerful or high compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess “cheerful” is part of it, but it’s more like… pumped up. Like I’ll be talking a mile a minute about how our little shop could become the main solar outfit in town, like it’s obviously going to happen if everyone just gets on board.\n\nAnd then if my wife is like, “Maybe slow down,” or one of the apprentices asks some normal question about the plan, I can snap at them. Not screaming or anything, but sharp—like they’re being stupid for not seeing it. Which, later, I know is not really fair.\n\nDoctor: During those pumped-up times, do you do things that are unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as risky, exactly. Like, I don’t think I’m out doing wild stuff just for the thrill of it or anything.\n\nI mean, I’ll make decisions faster than usual, maybe jump into ideas before I’ve really thought them through, but then again that’s kind of how work is sometimes—you’ve got a problem in front of you and you just have to solve it. Like last week we had this panel situation where the layout was all wrong, and I got thinking about how the supplier really should’ve caught that, because if they had just labeled the boxes better—\n\nSorry. What do you mean by risky? Like spending money, driving fast, that kind of thing? I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually irritable compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… irritable is probably the right word. Not, like, throwing things, but I get sharp with them, like they’re slowing me down or not getting it. And then later I’m thinking, why did I react like that? Which, later, I know is not really fair.\n\nDoctor: During those pumped-up times, do you do things that are unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as risky, exactly. Like last week we had this panel situation where the layout was all wrong, and I got thinking about how the supplier really should’ve caught that, because if they had just labeled the boxes better—\n\nSorry. What do you mean by risky? Like spending money, driving fast, that kind of thing? I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually irritable compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… irritable is probably the right word. It’s like I’ll be in this big, fired-up mood, talking about how our shop is about to take over solar work around here, and it all feels so obvious to me.\n\nBut then if my wife says something like, “Is that realistic?” or one of the apprentices asks a basic question about the plan, I can just flip kind of fast. Not, like, throwing things, but I get sharp with them, like they’re slowing me down or not getting it. And then later I’m thinking, why did I react like that? It wasn’t even a big deal.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, do you feel more confident or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, in the moment it feels less like “confidence” and more like, why wouldn’t I be able to do this?\n\nLike I’ll start thinking I can take on these bigger commercial jobs, train up a whole crew, have everyone running under me, basically build the shop into something way bigger right now. And I know, if I’m being honest, there are parts of that where my licensing and experience aren’t totally where they’d need to be yet.\n\nBut when I’m in that fired-up mode, that little practical part just gets kind of… quiet. It feels like I can figure it out as I go.\n\nDoctor: Have these fired-up periods caused serious problems at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “serious” at first, because work is always messy, you know? Scheduling is a nightmare anyway, especially when one job runs long and then the supplier drops stuff at the wrong site, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault—\n\nBut, yeah. Clients have started complaining. Like I missed details I usually wouldn’t miss, or I’ll have two things lined up in a way that doesn’t really make sense, scheduling-wise. And my apprentices… I can tell they don’t really trust my direction the same way. They double-check me more, or they hesitate.\n\nAt home it’s worse, honestly. My wife and I keep circling the same arguments about the mortgage and the business. She feels like I’m putting everything we’ve built at risk, and I get why she says it, even if in the moment I don’t want to hear it.\n\nDoctor: When you get into one of these fired-up periods, does it last at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to slice it up neatly into, like, “this week” and “that week.” It feels more like it’s been building for months.\n\nProbably since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and bought the equipment — so, what, about seven months now? That’s when everything started feeling more intense, like I had to keep pushing, keep proving the shop could handle it. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but it hasn’t just been a day or two thing.\n\nDoctor: During these fired-up periods, have you ever heard or seen things that other people said weren’t there, or believed something others thought was clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like seeing people who aren’t there or hearing voices, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ll get really locked onto an idea and other people will act like I’m being ridiculous, but it feels more like they’re not seeing the whole picture yet. Like with the shop, my wife will say, “That’s not realistic,” and then we end up arguing about numbers and timing and whether the suppliers can actually keep up — and half the time the suppliers are the real problem anyway, because they’ll tell you one thing on the phone and then the order shows up missing parts—\n\nSorry. No, I don’t think I’ve had, like, hallucinations or anything. Believing stuff… I guess people think I’m overestimating what I can pull off, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by clearly untrue.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like, I don’t think I’m out doing wild stuff just for the thrill of it or anything.\n\nI mean, I’ll make decisions faster than usual, maybe jump into ideas before I’ve really thought them through, but then again that’s kind of how work is sometimes—you’ve got a problem in front of you and you just have to solve it. Like last week we had this panel situation where the layout was all wrong, and I got thinking about how the supplier really should’ve caught that, because if they had just labeled the boxes better—\n\nSorry. What do you mean by risky? Like spending money, driving fast, that kind of thing? I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually irritable compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… irritable is probably the right word. It’s like I’ll be in this big, fired-up mood, talking about how our shop is about to take over solar work around here, and it all feels so obvious to me.\n\nBut then if my wife says something like, “Is that realistic?” or one of the apprentices asks a basic question about the plan, I can just flip kind of fast. Not, like, throwing things, but I get sharp with them, like they’re slowing me down or not getting it. And then later I’m thinking, why did I react like that? It wasn’t even a big deal.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, do you feel more confident or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, in the moment it feels less like “confidence” and more like, why wouldn’t I be able to do this?\n\nLike I’ll start thinking I can take on these bigger commercial jobs, train up a whole crew, have everyone running under me, basically build the shop into something way bigger right now. And I know, if I’m being honest, there are parts of that where my licensing and experience aren’t totally where they’d need to be yet.\n\nBut when I’m in that fired-up mode, that little practical part just gets kind of… quiet. It feels like I can figure it out as I go.\n\nDoctor: Have these fired-up periods caused serious problems at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “serious” at first, because work is always messy, you know? Scheduling is a nightmare anyway, especially when one job runs long and then the supplier drops stuff at the wrong site, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault—\n\nBut, yeah. Clients have started complaining. Like I missed details I usually wouldn’t miss, or I’ll have two things lined up in a way that doesn’t really make sense, scheduling-wise. And my apprentices… I can tell they don’t really trust my direction the same way. They double-check me more, or they hesitate.\n\nAt home it’s worse, honestly. My wife and I keep circling the same arguments about the mortgage and the business. She feels like I’m putting everything we’ve built at risk, and I get why she says it, even if in the moment I don’t want to hear it.\n\nDoctor: When you get into one of these fired-up periods, does it last at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to slice it up neatly into, like, “this week” and “that week.” It feels more like it’s been building for months.\n\nProbably since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and bought the equipment — so, what, about seven months now? That’s when everything started feeling more intense, like I had to keep pushing, keep proving the shop could handle it. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but it hasn’t just been a day or two thing.\n\nDoctor: During these fired-up periods, have you ever heard or seen things that other people said weren’t there, or believed something others thought was clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like seeing people who aren’t there or hearing voices, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ll get really locked onto an idea and other people will act like I’m being ridiculous, but it feels more like they’re not seeing the whole picture yet. Like with the shop, my wife will say, “That’s not realistic,” and then we end up arguing about numbers and timing and whether the suppliers can actually keep up — and half the time the suppliers are the real problem anyway, because they’ll tell you one thing on the phone and then the order shows up missing parts—\n\nSorry. Like I’ll be talking a mile a minute about how our little shop could become the main solar outfit in town, like it’s obviously going to happen if everyone just gets on board.\n\nAnd then if my wife is like, “Maybe slow down,” or one of the apprentices asks some normal question about the plan, I can snap at them. Not screaming or anything, but sharp—like they’re being stupid for not seeing it. Which, later, I know is not really fair.\n\nDoctor: During those pumped-up times, do you do things that are unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as risky, exactly. Like, I don’t think I’m out doing wild stuff just for the thrill of it or anything.\n\nI mean, I’ll make decisions faster than usual, maybe jump into ideas before I’ve really thought them through, but then again that’s kind of how work is sometimes—you’ve got a problem in front of you and you just have to solve it. Like last week we had this panel situation where the layout was all wrong, and I got thinking about how the supplier really should’ve caught that, because if they had just labeled the boxes better—\n\nSorry. What do you mean by risky? Like spending money, driving fast, that kind of thing? I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually irritable compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… irritable is probably the right word. It’s like I’ll be in this big, fired-up mood, talking about how our shop is about to take over solar work around here, and it all feels so obvious to me.\n\nBut then if my wife says something like, “Is that realistic?” or one of the apprentices asks a basic question about the plan, I can just flip kind of fast. Not, like, throwing things, but I get sharp with them, like they’re slowing me down or not getting it. And then later I’m thinking, why did I react like that? It wasn’t even a big deal.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, do you feel more confident or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, in the moment it feels less like “confidence” and more like, why wouldn’t I be able to do this?\n\nLike I’ll start thinking I can take on these bigger commercial jobs, train up a whole crew, have everyone running under me, basically build the shop into something way bigger right now. And I know, if I’m being honest, there are parts of that where my licensing and experience aren’t totally where they’d need to be yet.\n\nBut when I’m in that fired-up mode, that little practical part just gets kind of… quiet. It feels like I can figure it out as I go.\n\nDoctor: Have these fired-up periods caused serious problems at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “serious” at first, because work is always messy, you know? Scheduling is a nightmare anyway, especially when one job runs long and then the supplier drops stuff at the wrong site, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault—\n\nBut, yeah. Clients have started complaining. Like I missed details I usually wouldn’t miss, or I’ll have two things lined up in a way that doesn’t really make sense, scheduling-wise. And my apprentices… I can tell they don’t really trust my direction the same way. They double-check me more, or they hesitate.\n\nAt home it’s worse, honestly. My wife and I keep circling the same arguments about the mortgage and the business. She feels like I’m putting everything we’ve built at risk, and I get why she says it, even if in the moment I don’t want to hear it.\n\nDoctor: When you get into one of these fired-up periods, does it last at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to slice it up neatly into, like, “this week” and “that week.” It feels more like it’s been building for months.\n\nProbably since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and bought the equipment — so, what, about seven months now? That’s when everything started feeling more intense, like I had to keep pushing, keep proving the shop could handle it. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but it hasn’t just been a day or two thing.\n\nDoctor: During these fired-up periods, have you ever heard or seen things that other people said weren’t there, or believed something others thought was clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like seeing people who aren’t there or hearing voices, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ll get really locked onto an idea and other people will act like I’m being ridiculous, but it feels more like they’re not seeing the whole picture yet. Like with the shop, my wife will say, “That’s not realistic,” and then we end up arguing about numbers and timing and whether the suppliers can actually keep up — and half the time the suppliers are the real problem anyway, because they’ll tell you one thing on the phone and then the order shows up missing parts—\n\nSorry. No, I don’t think I’ve had, like, hallucinations or anything. Believing stuff… I guess people think I’m overestimating what I can pull off, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by clearly untrue.\n\nDoctor: During one of these fired-up periods, have you ever needed to go to the hospital because of how you were acting or feeling?\n\nPatient: No, not to the hospital. I mean, there have been times people made it sound like I was acting… I don’t know, out of control or not listening, but nobody’s taken me to the ER or anything like that.\n\nMy wife did say once I needed to “get help,” but that was in the middle of an argument, and we were already stressed about the shop and the bills and— yeah, no, I haven’t been hospitalized for it.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, do your thoughts race so fast that it’s hard to keep up with them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ve got a lot on my mind, sure — work stuff, money stuff, scheduling, whether a job’s going to get delayed because somebody didn’t order the right breaker or the permit office is dragging their feet…\n\nBut “racing” like I can’t keep up with them? I don’t know. Maybe it feels busy in my head, but I’ve also always had a lot to juggle. My wife says I jump from one thing to another when I’m talking, but that’s different, right? Or maybe that’s what you mean. I’m not totally sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I guess… mostly? I mean, that’s hard to pin down because what’s “usual,” you know? Like my wife would probably say I’m never completely easy to live with, but I think I settle back into just doing work, coming home, dealing with the normal stuff.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.9, F90.0, F41.9, F43.9, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it's good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… mostly because work’s been getting messy. I’m an electrician, and lately I can’t stay on one estimate or code form long enough to finish it. Like, I’ll be trying to write up one job, then a tool drops or my phone buzzes, or I’ll suddenly think of some better setup for the workshop, and I’m gone. Then I come back and I’ve mixed up details between jobs.\n\nIt sounds small when I say it, but it’s been happening enough that I figured I should probably talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this kind of trouble focusing when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I remember school being… school. I wasn’t the kid who loved sitting still for math or anything, but I don’t know if that was a “focus problem” or just being a kid. I did better in classes where I could use my hands—shop class, fixing stuff, that kind of thing.\n\nMy mom would probably remember better. She keeps all this old school paperwork in a box somewhere, like report cards and random certificates, even one from a science fair where I made this terrible little motor thing that barely worked. But as far as whether it was like what’s happening now… I don’t know. I can’t really say.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s been happening. For, I don’t know, months now I’ll sleep maybe three or four hours and then I’m just up before dawn like my brain’s already clocked in. I’ll start pricing materials or answering emails, or I’ll sketch out some wiring setup or system idea like it’s totally normal to be doing that at 4:30 in the morning.\n\nAnd the weird part is I don’t always feel wrecked from it. Like if I only slept that much after a long job, normally I’d be useless, but lately I’ll pop up feeling like I got a full night, at least at first. Then I get into the day and there’s all this stuff I’m trying to do, and—sorry, I’m getting off track—but yeah, much less sleep and still feeling pretty energized.\n\nDoctor: During these times when you sleep only three or four hours, do you also feel unusually cheerful or high compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess “cheerful” is part of it, but it’s more like… pumped up. Like I’ll be talking a mile a minute about how our little shop could become the main solar outfit in town, like it’s obviously going to happen if everyone just gets on board.\n\nAnd then if my wife is like, “Maybe slow down,” or one of the apprentices asks some normal question about the plan, I can snap at them. Not screaming or anything, but sharp—like they’re being stupid for not seeing it. Which, later, I know is not really fair.\n\nDoctor: During those pumped-up times, do you do things that are unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as risky, exactly. Like, I don’t think I’m out doing wild stuff just for the thrill of it or anything.\n\nI mean, I’ll make decisions faster than usual, maybe jump into ideas before I’ve really thought them through, but then again that’s kind of how work is sometimes—you’ve got a problem in front of you and you just have to solve it. Like last week we had this panel situation where the layout was all wrong, and I got thinking about how the supplier really should’ve caught that, because if they had just labeled the boxes better—\n\nSorry. What do you mean by risky? Like spending money, driving fast, that kind of thing? I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually irritable compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… irritable is probably the right word. It’s like I’ll be in this big, fired-up mood, talking about how our shop is about to take over solar work around here, and it all feels so obvious to me.\n\nBut then if my wife says something like, “Is that realistic?” or one of the apprentices asks a basic question about the plan, I can just flip kind of fast. Not, like, throwing things, but I get sharp with them, like they’re slowing me down or not getting it. And then later I’m thinking, why did I react like that? It wasn’t even a big deal.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, do you feel more confident or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, in the moment it feels less like “confidence” and more like, why wouldn’t I be able to do this?\n\nLike I’ll start thinking I can take on these bigger commercial jobs, train up a whole crew, have everyone running under me, basically build the shop into something way bigger right now. And I know, if I’m being honest, there are parts of that where my licensing and experience aren’t totally where they’d need to be yet.\n\nBut when I’m in that fired-up mode, that little practical part just gets kind of… quiet. It feels like I can figure it out as I go.\n\nDoctor: Have these fired-up periods caused serious problems at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “serious” at first, because work is always messy, you know? Scheduling is a nightmare anyway, especially when one job runs long and then the supplier drops stuff at the wrong site, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault—\n\nBut, yeah. Clients have started complaining. Like I missed details I usually wouldn’t miss, or I’ll have two things lined up in a way that doesn’t really make sense, scheduling-wise. And my apprentices… I can tell they don’t really trust my direction the same way. They double-check me more, or they hesitate.\n\nAt home it’s worse, honestly. My wife and I keep circling the same arguments about the mortgage and the business. She feels like I’m putting everything we’ve built at risk, and I get why she says it, even if in the moment I don’t want to hear it.\n\nDoctor: When you get into one of these fired-up periods, does it last at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to slice it up neatly into, like, “this week” and “that week.” It feels more like it’s been building for months.\n\nProbably since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and bought the equipment — so, what, about seven months now? That’s when everything started feeling more intense, like I had to keep pushing, keep proving the shop could handle it. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but it hasn’t just been a day or two thing.\n\nDoctor: During these fired-up periods, have you ever heard or seen things that other people said weren’t there, or believed something others thought was clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like seeing people who aren’t there or hearing voices, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ll get really locked onto an idea and other people will act like I’m being ridiculous, but it feels more like they’re not seeing the whole picture yet. Like with the shop, my wife will say, “That’s not realistic,” and then we end up arguing about numbers and timing and whether the suppliers can actually keep up — and half the time the suppliers are the real problem anyway, because they’ll tell you one thing on the phone and then the order shows up missing parts—\n\nSorry. No, I don’t think I’ve had, like, hallucinations or anything. Believing stuff… I guess people think I’m overestimating what I can pull off, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by clearly untrue.\n\nDoctor: During one of these fired-up periods, have you ever needed to go to the hospital because of how you were acting or feeling?\n\nPatient: No, not to the hospital. I mean, there have been times people made it sound like I was acting… I don’t know, out of control or not listening, but nobody’s taken me to the ER or anything like that.\n\nMy wife did say once I needed to “get help,” but that was in the middle of an argument, and we were already stressed about the shop and the bills and— yeah, no, I haven’t been hospitalized for it.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, do your thoughts race so fast that it’s hard to keep up with them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ve got a lot on my mind, sure — work stuff, money stuff, scheduling, whether a job’s going to get delayed because somebody didn’t order the right breaker or the permit office is dragging their feet…\n\nBut “racing” like I can’t keep up with them? I don’t know. Maybe it feels busy in my head, but I’ve also always had a lot to juggle. My wife says I jump from one thing to another when I’m talking, but that’s different, right? Or maybe that’s what you mean. I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Between these fired-up periods, do you return to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly? I mean, that’s hard to pin down because what’s “usual,” you know? Like my wife would probably say I’m never completely easy to live with, but I think I settle back into just doing work, coming home, dealing with the normal stuff.\n\nIt’s not like I’m keeping track on a calendar or anything. Sometimes after a stretch like that I’m tired or things are messy because I’ve taken on too much, and then you’re dealing with the fallout, which can look like you’re still not yourself. Like if a job runs over budget, you don’t just wake up the next week and everything’s fixed.\n\nBut yeah… I think I go back to more or less normal. I don’t know exactly how to answer it better than that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like if a job runs over budget, you don’t just wake up the next week and everything’s fixed.\n\nBut yeah… I think I go back to more or less normal. I don’t know exactly how to answer it better than that.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you’re in one of these fired-up periods, do you take on a lot more projects or work than you normally would?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / electrician / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Increased_Activity\nmanifestation: \"I’ve been going nonstop from job sites to bids to rewiring test panels in the workshop, starting new home-automation ideas before the last one is done because sitting still feels like wasting time.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, yes, probably. When I’m like that I’ll say yes to way more than I normally should. I’ll go from a job site straight to looking at a bid, then back to the workshop messing with test panels or wiring something up, and then I’ll get some idea about home automation—like, “Oh, this would be easy, I can build that”—even though the last thing isn’t finished yet.

It doesn’t feel like too much at the time. It feels like sitting still is just… wasting time, you know? Like there’s momentum and I don’t want to lose it. Then later it’s like, okay, why did I start three different things when one would’ve been enough.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.9, F90.0, F41.9, F43.9, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it's good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… mostly because work’s been getting messy. I’m an electrician, and lately I can’t stay on one estimate or code form long enough to finish it. Like, I’ll be trying to write up one job, then a tool drops or my phone buzzes, or I’ll suddenly think of some better setup for the workshop, and I’m gone. Then I come back and I’ve mixed up details between jobs.\n\nIt sounds small when I say it, but it’s been happening enough that I figured I should probably talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this kind of trouble focusing when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I remember school being… school. I wasn’t the kid who loved sitting still for math or anything, but I don’t know if that was a “focus problem” or just being a kid. I did better in classes where I could use my hands—shop class, fixing stuff, that kind of thing.\n\nMy mom would probably remember better. She keeps all this old school paperwork in a box somewhere, like report cards and random certificates, even one from a science fair where I made this terrible little motor thing that barely worked. But as far as whether it was like what’s happening now… I don’t know. I can’t really say.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s been happening. For, I don’t know, months now I’ll sleep maybe three or four hours and then I’m just up before dawn like my brain’s already clocked in. I’ll start pricing materials or answering emails, or I’ll sketch out some wiring setup or system idea like it’s totally normal to be doing that at 4:30 in the morning.\n\nAnd the weird part is I don’t always feel wrecked from it. Like if I only slept that much after a long job, normally I’d be useless, but lately I’ll pop up feeling like I got a full night, at least at first. Then I get into the day and there’s all this stuff I’m trying to do, and—sorry, I’m getting off track—but yeah, much less sleep and still feeling pretty energized.\n\nDoctor: During these times when you sleep only three or four hours, do you also feel unusually cheerful or high compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess “cheerful” is part of it, but it’s more like… pumped up. Like I’ll be talking a mile a minute about how our little shop could become the main solar outfit in town, like it’s obviously going to happen if everyone just gets on board.\n\nAnd then if my wife is like, “Maybe slow down,” or one of the apprentices asks some normal question about the plan, I can snap at them. Not screaming or anything, but sharp—like they’re being stupid for not seeing it. Which, later, I know is not really fair.\n\nDoctor: During those pumped-up times, do you do things that are unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as risky, exactly. Like, I don’t think I’m out doing wild stuff just for the thrill of it or anything.\n\nI mean, I’ll make decisions faster than usual, maybe jump into ideas before I’ve really thought them through, but then again that’s kind of how work is sometimes—you’ve got a problem in front of you and you just have to solve it. Like last week we had this panel situation where the layout was all wrong, and I got thinking about how the supplier really should’ve caught that, because if they had just labeled the boxes better—\n\nSorry. What do you mean by risky? Like spending money, driving fast, that kind of thing? I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually irritable compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… irritable is probably the right word. It’s like I’ll be in this big, fired-up mood, talking about how our shop is about to take over solar work around here, and it all feels so obvious to me.\n\nBut then if my wife says something like, “Is that realistic?” or one of the apprentices asks a basic question about the plan, I can just flip kind of fast. Not, like, throwing things, but I get sharp with them, like they’re slowing me down or not getting it. And then later I’m thinking, why did I react like that? It wasn’t even a big deal.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, do you feel more confident or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, in the moment it feels less like “confidence” and more like, why wouldn’t I be able to do this?\n\nLike I’ll start thinking I can take on these bigger commercial jobs, train up a whole crew, have everyone running under me, basically build the shop into something way bigger right now. And I know, if I’m being honest, there are parts of that where my licensing and experience aren’t totally where they’d need to be yet.\n\nBut when I’m in that fired-up mode, that little practical part just gets kind of… quiet. It feels like I can figure it out as I go.\n\nDoctor: Have these fired-up periods caused serious problems at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “serious” at first, because work is always messy, you know? Scheduling is a nightmare anyway, especially when one job runs long and then the supplier drops stuff at the wrong site, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault—\n\nBut, yeah. Clients have started complaining. Like I missed details I usually wouldn’t miss, or I’ll have two things lined up in a way that doesn’t really make sense, scheduling-wise. And my apprentices… I can tell they don’t really trust my direction the same way. They double-check me more, or they hesitate.\n\nAt home it’s worse, honestly. My wife and I keep circling the same arguments about the mortgage and the business. She feels like I’m putting everything we’ve built at risk, and I get why she says it, even if in the moment I don’t want to hear it.\n\nDoctor: When you get into one of these fired-up periods, does it last at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to slice it up neatly into, like, “this week” and “that week.” It feels more like it’s been building for months.\n\nProbably since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and bought the equipment — so, what, about seven months now? That’s when everything started feeling more intense, like I had to keep pushing, keep proving the shop could handle it. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but it hasn’t just been a day or two thing.\n\nDoctor: During these fired-up periods, have you ever heard or seen things that other people said weren’t there, or believed something others thought was clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like seeing people who aren’t there or hearing voices, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ll get really locked onto an idea and other people will act like I’m being ridiculous, but it feels more like they’re not seeing the whole picture yet. Like with the shop, my wife will say, “That’s not realistic,” and then we end up arguing about numbers and timing and whether the suppliers can actually keep up — and half the time the suppliers are the real problem anyway, because they’ll tell you one thing on the phone and then the order shows up missing parts—\n\nSorry. No, I don’t think I’ve had, like, hallucinations or anything. Believing stuff… I guess people think I’m overestimating what I can pull off, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by clearly untrue.\n\nDoctor: During one of these fired-up periods, have you ever needed to go to the hospital because of how you were acting or feeling?\n\nPatient: No, not to the hospital. I mean, there have been times people made it sound like I was acting… I don’t know, out of control or not listening, but nobody’s taken me to the ER or anything like that.\n\nMy wife did say once I needed to “get help,” but that was in the middle of an argument, and we were already stressed about the shop and the bills and— yeah, no, I haven’t been hospitalized for it.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, do your thoughts race so fast that it’s hard to keep up with them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ve got a lot on my mind, sure — work stuff, money stuff, scheduling, whether a job’s going to get delayed because somebody didn’t order the right breaker or the permit office is dragging their feet…\n\nBut “racing” like I can’t keep up with them? I don’t know. Maybe it feels busy in my head, but I’ve also always had a lot to juggle. My wife says I jump from one thing to another when I’m talking, but that’s different, right? Or maybe that’s what you mean. I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Between these fired-up periods, do you return to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly? I mean, that’s hard to pin down because what’s “usual,” you know? Like my wife would probably say I’m never completely easy to live with, but I think I settle back into just doing work, coming home, dealing with the normal stuff.\n\nIt’s not like I’m keeping track on a calendar or anything. Sometimes after a stretch like that I’m tired or things are messy because I’ve taken on too much, and then you’re dealing with the fallout, which can look like you’re still not yourself. Like if a job runs over budget, you don’t just wake up the next week and everything’s fixed.\n\nBut yeah… I think I go back to more or less normal. I don’t know exactly how to answer it better than that.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in one of these fired-up periods, do you take on a lot more projects or work than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, probably. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’ll get on a roll and it’s like I can’t just do the one thing. I’ll go from job sites to checking out new bids, then end up in the workshop rewiring some test panel or messing with some setup that probably could’ve waited.

At the time it feels productive, like I’m using the energy right. Sitting still feels like wasting time. Then later I look around and it’s like… okay, I started a lot. Maybe too much.

Not screaming or anything, but sharp—like they’re being stupid for not seeing it. Which, later, I know is not really fair.\n\nDoctor: During those pumped-up times, do you do things that are unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as risky, exactly. Like, I don’t think I’m out doing wild stuff just for the thrill of it or anything.\n\nI mean, I’ll make decisions faster than usual, maybe jump into ideas before I’ve really thought them through, but then again that’s kind of how work is sometimes—you’ve got a problem in front of you and you just have to solve it. Like last week we had this panel situation where the layout was all wrong, and I got thinking about how the supplier really should’ve caught that, because if they had just labeled the boxes better—\n\nSorry. What do you mean by risky? Like spending money, driving fast, that kind of thing? I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually irritable compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… irritable is probably the right word. It’s like I’ll be in this big, fired-up mood, talking about how our shop is about to take over solar work around here, and it all feels so obvious to me.\n\nBut then if my wife says something like, “Is that realistic?” or one of the apprentices asks a basic question about the plan, I can just flip kind of fast. Not, like, throwing things, but I get sharp with them, like they’re slowing me down or not getting it. And then later I’m thinking, why did I react like that? It wasn’t even a big deal.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, do you feel more confident or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, in the moment it feels less like “confidence” and more like, why wouldn’t I be able to do this?\n\nLike I’ll start thinking I can take on these bigger commercial jobs, train up a whole crew, have everyone running under me, basically build the shop into something way bigger right now. And I know, if I’m being honest, there are parts of that where my licensing and experience aren’t totally where they’d need to be yet.\n\nBut when I’m in that fired-up mode, that little practical part just gets kind of… quiet. It feels like I can figure it out as I go.\n\nDoctor: Have these fired-up periods caused serious problems at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “serious” at first, because work is always messy, you know? Scheduling is a nightmare anyway, especially when one job runs long and then the supplier drops stuff at the wrong site, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault—\n\nBut, yeah. Clients have started complaining. Like I missed details I usually wouldn’t miss, or I’ll have two things lined up in a way that doesn’t really make sense, scheduling-wise. And my apprentices… I can tell they don’t really trust my direction the same way. They double-check me more, or they hesitate.\n\nAt home it’s worse, honestly. My wife and I keep circling the same arguments about the mortgage and the business. She feels like I’m putting everything we’ve built at risk, and I get why she says it, even if in the moment I don’t want to hear it.\n\nDoctor: When you get into one of these fired-up periods, does it last at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to slice it up neatly into, like, “this week” and “that week.” It feels more like it’s been building for months.\n\nProbably since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and bought the equipment — so, what, about seven months now? That’s when everything started feeling more intense, like I had to keep pushing, keep proving the shop could handle it. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but it hasn’t just been a day or two thing.\n\nDoctor: During these fired-up periods, have you ever heard or seen things that other people said weren’t there, or believed something others thought was clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like seeing people who aren’t there or hearing voices, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ll get really locked onto an idea and other people will act like I’m being ridiculous, but it feels more like they’re not seeing the whole picture yet. Like with the shop, my wife will say, “That’s not realistic,” and then we end up arguing about numbers and timing and whether the suppliers can actually keep up — and half the time the suppliers are the real problem anyway, because they’ll tell you one thing on the phone and then the order shows up missing parts—\n\nSorry. No, I don’t think I’ve had, like, hallucinations or anything. I mean, there have been times people made it sound like I was acting… I don’t know, out of control or not listening, but nobody’s taken me to the ER or anything like that.\n\nMy wife did say once I needed to “get help,” but that was in the middle of an argument, and we were already stressed about the shop and the bills and— yeah, no, I haven’t been hospitalized for it.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, do your thoughts race so fast that it’s hard to keep up with them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I talk a lot anyway, depending who you ask. My wife would probably roll her eyes at that.

I don’t know if it’s much more. I mean, I’ll buy stuff for projects sometimes — parts, tools, little smart switches or sensors, whatever — but with electrical work there’s always some reason you can justify buying a thing, you know?

I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually irritable compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… irritable is probably the right word. It’s like I’ll be in this big, fired-up mood, talking about how our shop is about to take over solar work around here, and it all feels so obvious to me.\n\nBut then if my wife says something like, “Is that realistic?” or one of the apprentices asks a basic question about the plan, I can just flip kind of fast. Not, like, throwing things, but I get sharp with them, like they’re slowing me down or not getting it. And then later I’m thinking, why did I react like that? It wasn’t even a big deal.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, do you feel more confident or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, in the moment it feels less like “confidence” and more like, why wouldn’t I be able to do this?\n\nLike I’ll start thinking I can take on these bigger commercial jobs, train up a whole crew, have everyone running under me, basically build the shop into something way bigger right now. And I know, if I’m being honest, there are parts of that where my licensing and experience aren’t totally where they’d need to be yet.\n\nBut when I’m in that fired-up mode, that little practical part just gets kind of… quiet. It feels like I can figure it out as I go.\n\nDoctor: Have these fired-up periods caused serious problems at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “serious” at first, because work is always messy, you know? Scheduling is a nightmare anyway, especially when one job runs long and then the supplier drops stuff at the wrong site, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault—\n\nBut, yeah. Clients have started complaining. Like I missed details I usually wouldn’t miss, or I’ll have two things lined up in a way that doesn’t really make sense, scheduling-wise. And my apprentices… I can tell they don’t really trust my direction the same way. They double-check me more, or they hesitate.\n\nAt home it’s worse, honestly. My wife and I keep circling the same arguments about the mortgage and the business. She feels like I’m putting everything we’ve built at risk, and I get why she says it, even if in the moment I don’t want to hear it.\n\nDoctor: When you get into one of these fired-up periods, does it last at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to slice it up neatly into, like, “this week” and “that week.” It feels more like it’s been building for months.\n\nProbably since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and bought the equipment — so, what, about seven months now? That’s when everything started feeling more intense, like I had to keep pushing, keep proving the shop could handle it. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but it hasn’t just been a day or two thing.\n\nDoctor: During these fired-up periods, have you ever heard or seen things that other people said weren’t there, or believed something others thought was clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like seeing people who aren’t there or hearing voices, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ll get really locked onto an idea and other people will act like I’m being ridiculous, but it feels more like they’re not seeing the whole picture yet. Like with the shop, my wife will say, “That’s not realistic,” and then we end up arguing about numbers and timing and whether the suppliers can actually keep up — and half the time the suppliers are the real problem anyway, because they’ll tell you one thing on the phone and then the order shows up missing parts—\n\nSorry. No, I don’t think I’ve had, like, hallucinations or anything. Believing stuff… I guess people think I’m overestimating what I can pull off, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by clearly untrue.\n\nDoctor: During one of these fired-up periods, have you ever needed to go to the hospital because of how you were acting or feeling?\n\nPatient: No, not to the hospital. I mean, there have been times people made it sound like I was acting… I don’t know, out of control or not listening, but nobody’s taken me to the ER or anything like that.\n\nMy wife did say once I needed to “get help,” but that was in the middle of an argument, and we were already stressed about the shop and the bills and— yeah, no, I haven’t been hospitalized for it.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, do your thoughts race so fast that it’s hard to keep up with them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ve got a lot on my mind, sure — work stuff, money stuff, scheduling, whether a job’s going to get delayed because somebody didn’t order the right breaker or the permit office is dragging their feet…\n\nBut “racing” like I can’t keep up with them? I don’t know. Maybe it feels busy in my head, but I’ve also always had a lot to juggle. My wife says I jump from one thing to another when I’m talking, but that’s different, right? Or maybe that’s what you mean. I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Between these fired-up periods, do you return to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly? I mean, that’s hard to pin down because what’s “usual,” you know? Like my wife would probably say I’m never completely easy to live with, but I think I settle back into just doing work, coming home, dealing with the normal stuff.\n\nIt’s not like I’m keeping track on a calendar or anything. Sometimes after a stretch like that I’m tired or things are messy because I’ve taken on too much, and then you’re dealing with the fallout, which can look like you’re still not yourself. Like if a job runs over budget, you don’t just wake up the next week and everything’s fixed.\n\nBut yeah… I think I go back to more or less normal. I don’t know exactly how to answer it better than that.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in one of these fired-up periods, do you take on a lot more projects or work than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, probably. When I’m like that I’ll say yes to way more than I normally should. I’ll go from a job site straight to looking at a bid, then back to the workshop messing with test panels or wiring something up, and then I’ll get some idea about home automation—like, “Oh, this would be easy, I can build that”—even though the last thing isn’t finished yet.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like too much at the time. It feels like sitting still is just… wasting time, you know? Like there’s momentum and I don’t want to lose it. Then later it’s like, okay, why did I start three different things when one would’ve been enough.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up periods, do you get much more done or start a lot more purposeful work than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’ll get on a roll and it’s like I can’t just do the one thing. I’ll go from job sites to checking out new bids, then end up in the workshop rewiring some test panel or messing with some setup that probably could’ve waited.\n\nAnd then I’ll get this idea for some home-automation thing — like lights or sensors or whatever — and I’m starting that before I’ve even finished the last project. At the time it feels productive, like I’m using the energy right. Sitting still feels like wasting time. Then later I look around and it’s like… okay, I started a lot. Maybe too much.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up periods, do you talk much more than usual or feel hard to stop once you get going?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I talk a lot anyway, depending who you ask. My wife would probably roll her eyes at that.\n\nWhen I’m working, especially on a job, you kind of have to talk through things — like if you’re explaining to an apprentice why something’s wired wrong, or trying to get a contractor to understand why their timeline makes no sense. And some people just don’t listen the first time, so then you end up saying it three different ways.\n\nBut hard to stop… I’m not sure I notice it like that. I guess once I get into explaining something I can keep going, but I don’t know if that’s one of those periods or just me being me.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up periods, have you spent much more money than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s much more. I mean, I’ll buy stuff for projects sometimes — parts, tools, little smart switches or sensors, whatever — but with electrical work there’s always some reason you can justify buying a thing, you know?\n\nLike, if I’m at the supply house, it’s easy to be like, “Well, I’ll use that eventually,” and then you get home and realize you already had one in a bin somewhere. I’ve got bins of parts that… honestly I should probably label better, because then I wouldn’t be buying duplicates.\n\nBut as far as like, going crazy with money or something, I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it that closely. My wife might have a better answer than me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I mean, I’ll get on a roll and it’s like I can’t just do the one thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, “can’t function” sounds kind of dramatic, but it’s definitely been affecting things.

At work, I’ll think I’ve got everything handled, and then a client’s like, “You said you were coming Tuesday,” or there’s some detail I missed that I normally wouldn’t. And that’s embarrassing, because I’ve built a lot on being the guy who catches that stuff. Even my apprentices… I can tell they don’t trust my direction the same way sometimes, like they’re double-checking me more.

And at home it’s… yeah, it’s a problem. My wife and I keep getting into it about the mortgage, the business, all of that. She thinks I’m risking what we’ve built, and I get why she says it, even if in the moment I feel like I’m just trying to keep things moving.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.1, F31.9, F90.0, F41.9, F43.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it's good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… mostly because work’s been getting messy. I’m an electrician, and lately I can’t stay on one estimate or code form long enough to finish it. Like, I’ll be trying to write up one job, then a tool drops or my phone buzzes, or I’ll suddenly think of some better setup for the workshop, and I’m gone. Then I come back and I’ve mixed up details between jobs.\n\nIt sounds small when I say it, but it’s been happening enough that I figured I should probably talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this kind of trouble focusing when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I remember school being… school. I wasn’t the kid who loved sitting still for math or anything, but I don’t know if that was a “focus problem” or just being a kid. I did better in classes where I could use my hands—shop class, fixing stuff, that kind of thing.\n\nMy mom would probably remember better. She keeps all this old school paperwork in a box somewhere, like report cards and random certificates, even one from a science fair where I made this terrible little motor thing that barely worked. But as far as whether it was like what’s happening now… I don’t know. I can’t really say.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s been happening. For, I don’t know, months now I’ll sleep maybe three or four hours and then I’m just up before dawn like my brain’s already clocked in. I’ll start pricing materials or answering emails, or I’ll sketch out some wiring setup or system idea like it’s totally normal to be doing that at 4:30 in the morning.\n\nAnd the weird part is I don’t always feel wrecked from it. Like if I only slept that much after a long job, normally I’d be useless, but lately I’ll pop up feeling like I got a full night, at least at first. Then I get into the day and there’s all this stuff I’m trying to do, and—sorry, I’m getting off track—but yeah, much less sleep and still feeling pretty energized.\n\nDoctor: During these times when you sleep only three or four hours, do you also feel unusually cheerful or high compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess “cheerful” is part of it, but it’s more like… pumped up. Like I’ll be talking a mile a minute about how our little shop could become the main solar outfit in town, like it’s obviously going to happen if everyone just gets on board.\n\nAnd then if my wife is like, “Maybe slow down,” or one of the apprentices asks some normal question about the plan, I can snap at them. Not screaming or anything, but sharp—like they’re being stupid for not seeing it. Which, later, I know is not really fair.\n\nDoctor: During those pumped-up times, do you do things that are unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as risky, exactly. Like, I don’t think I’m out doing wild stuff just for the thrill of it or anything.\n\nI mean, I’ll make decisions faster than usual, maybe jump into ideas before I’ve really thought them through, but then again that’s kind of how work is sometimes—you’ve got a problem in front of you and you just have to solve it. Like last week we had this panel situation where the layout was all wrong, and I got thinking about how the supplier really should’ve caught that, because if they had just labeled the boxes better—\n\nSorry. What do you mean by risky? Like spending money, driving fast, that kind of thing? I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually irritable compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… irritable is probably the right word. It’s like I’ll be in this big, fired-up mood, talking about how our shop is about to take over solar work around here, and it all feels so obvious to me.\n\nBut then if my wife says something like, “Is that realistic?” or one of the apprentices asks a basic question about the plan, I can just flip kind of fast. Not, like, throwing things, but I get sharp with them, like they’re slowing me down or not getting it. And then later I’m thinking, why did I react like that? It wasn’t even a big deal.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, do you feel more confident or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, in the moment it feels less like “confidence” and more like, why wouldn’t I be able to do this?\n\nLike I’ll start thinking I can take on these bigger commercial jobs, train up a whole crew, have everyone running under me, basically build the shop into something way bigger right now. And I know, if I’m being honest, there are parts of that where my licensing and experience aren’t totally where they’d need to be yet.\n\nBut when I’m in that fired-up mode, that little practical part just gets kind of… quiet. It feels like I can figure it out as I go.\n\nDoctor: Have these fired-up periods caused serious problems at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “serious” at first, because work is always messy, you know? Scheduling is a nightmare anyway, especially when one job runs long and then the supplier drops stuff at the wrong site, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault—\n\nBut, yeah. Clients have started complaining. Like I missed details I usually wouldn’t miss, or I’ll have two things lined up in a way that doesn’t really make sense, scheduling-wise. And my apprentices… I can tell they don’t really trust my direction the same way. They double-check me more, or they hesitate.\n\nAt home it’s worse, honestly. My wife and I keep circling the same arguments about the mortgage and the business. She feels like I’m putting everything we’ve built at risk, and I get why she says it, even if in the moment I don’t want to hear it.\n\nDoctor: When you get into one of these fired-up periods, does it last at least a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to slice it up neatly into, like, “this week” and “that week.” It feels more like it’s been building for months.\n\nProbably since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and bought the equipment — so, what, about seven months now? That’s when everything started feeling more intense, like I had to keep pushing, keep proving the shop could handle it. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but it hasn’t just been a day or two thing.\n\nDoctor: During these fired-up periods, have you ever heard or seen things that other people said weren’t there, or believed something others thought was clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like seeing people who aren’t there or hearing voices, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ll get really locked onto an idea and other people will act like I’m being ridiculous, but it feels more like they’re not seeing the whole picture yet. Like with the shop, my wife will say, “That’s not realistic,” and then we end up arguing about numbers and timing and whether the suppliers can actually keep up — and half the time the suppliers are the real problem anyway, because they’ll tell you one thing on the phone and then the order shows up missing parts—\n\nSorry. No, I don’t think I’ve had, like, hallucinations or anything. Believing stuff… I guess people think I’m overestimating what I can pull off, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by clearly untrue.\n\nDoctor: During one of these fired-up periods, have you ever needed to go to the hospital because of how you were acting or feeling?\n\nPatient: No, not to the hospital. I mean, there have been times people made it sound like I was acting… I don’t know, out of control or not listening, but nobody’s taken me to the ER or anything like that.\n\nMy wife did say once I needed to “get help,” but that was in the middle of an argument, and we were already stressed about the shop and the bills and— yeah, no, I haven’t been hospitalized for it.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, do your thoughts race so fast that it’s hard to keep up with them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ve got a lot on my mind, sure — work stuff, money stuff, scheduling, whether a job’s going to get delayed because somebody didn’t order the right breaker or the permit office is dragging their feet…\n\nBut “racing” like I can’t keep up with them? I don’t know. Maybe it feels busy in my head, but I’ve also always had a lot to juggle. My wife says I jump from one thing to another when I’m talking, but that’s different, right? Or maybe that’s what you mean. I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Between these fired-up periods, do you return to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly? I mean, that’s hard to pin down because what’s “usual,” you know? Like my wife would probably say I’m never completely easy to live with, but I think I settle back into just doing work, coming home, dealing with the normal stuff.\n\nIt’s not like I’m keeping track on a calendar or anything. Sometimes after a stretch like that I’m tired or things are messy because I’ve taken on too much, and then you’re dealing with the fallout, which can look like you’re still not yourself. Like if a job runs over budget, you don’t just wake up the next week and everything’s fixed.\n\nBut yeah… I think I go back to more or less normal. I don’t know exactly how to answer it better than that.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in one of these fired-up periods, do you take on a lot more projects or work than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, probably. When I’m like that I’ll say yes to way more than I normally should. I’ll go from a job site straight to looking at a bid, then back to the workshop messing with test panels or wiring something up, and then I’ll get some idea about home automation—like, “Oh, this would be easy, I can build that”—even though the last thing isn’t finished yet.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like too much at the time. It feels like sitting still is just… wasting time, you know? Like there’s momentum and I don’t want to lose it. Then later it’s like, okay, why did I start three different things when one would’ve been enough.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up periods, do you get much more done or start a lot more purposeful work than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’ll get on a roll and it’s like I can’t just do the one thing. I’ll go from job sites to checking out new bids, then end up in the workshop rewiring some test panel or messing with some setup that probably could’ve waited.\n\nAnd then I’ll get this idea for some home-automation thing — like lights or sensors or whatever — and I’m starting that before I’ve even finished the last project. At the time it feels productive, like I’m using the energy right. Sitting still feels like wasting time. Then later I look around and it’s like… okay, I started a lot. Maybe too much.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up periods, do you talk much more than usual or feel hard to stop once you get going?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I talk a lot anyway, depending who you ask. My wife would probably roll her eyes at that.\n\nWhen I’m working, especially on a job, you kind of have to talk through things — like if you’re explaining to an apprentice why something’s wired wrong, or trying to get a contractor to understand why their timeline makes no sense. And some people just don’t listen the first time, so then you end up saying it three different ways.\n\nBut hard to stop… I’m not sure I notice it like that. I guess once I get into explaining something I can keep going, but I don’t know if that’s one of those periods or just me being me.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up periods, have you spent much more money than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s much more. I mean, I’ll buy stuff for projects sometimes — parts, tools, little smart switches or sensors, whatever — but with electrical work there’s always some reason you can justify buying a thing, you know?\n\nLike, if I’m at the supply house, it’s easy to be like, “Well, I’ll use that eventually,” and then you get home and realize you already had one in a bin somewhere. I’ve got bins of parts that… honestly I should probably label better, because then I wouldn’t be buying duplicates.\n\nBut as far as like, going crazy with money or something, I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it that closely. Like my wife would probably say I’m never completely easy to live with, but I think I settle back into just doing work, coming home, dealing with the normal stuff.\n\nIt’s not like I’m keeping track on a calendar or anything. Sometimes after a stretch like that I’m tired or things are messy because I’ve taken on too much, and then you’re dealing with the fallout, which can look like you’re still not yourself. Like if a job runs over budget, you don’t just wake up the next week and everything’s fixed.\n\nBut yeah… I think I go back to more or less normal. I don’t know exactly how to answer it better than that.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in one of these fired-up periods, do you take on a lot more projects or work than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, probably. Completely certain? I don’t know if I’d say impossible, exactly. It’s more like… during those times I get this feeling like I could take on bigger commercial jobs, like right now, and build out a whole crew and train them up.

And I mean, part of that isn’t crazy — I know the work, I’ve been doing electrical a long time, and half the problem in this trade is people not being taught right in the first place. I had this one guy years ago who showed me a panel layout that was just… anyway, that kind of thing makes me think I could do it better.

My wife will point that out, or someone will say, “You’re not set up for that yet,” and in the moment I’m like, no, you just don’t see it. I don’t know. Maybe it feels busy in my head, but I’ve also always had a lot to juggle. My wife says I jump from one thing to another when I’m talking, but that’s different, right? Sometimes after a stretch like that I’m tired or things are messy because I’ve taken on too much, and then you’re dealing with the fallout, which can look like you’re still not yourself. Like if a job runs over budget, you don’t just wake up the next week and everything’s fixed.\n\nBut yeah… I think I go back to more or less normal. I don’t know exactly how to answer it better than that.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in one of these fired-up periods, do you take on a lot more projects or work than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, probably. When I’m like that I’ll say yes to way more than I normally should. I’ll go from a job site straight to looking at a bid, then back to the workshop messing with test panels or wiring something up, and then I’ll get some idea about home automation—like, “Oh, this would be easy, I can build that”—even though the last thing isn’t finished yet.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like too much at the time. My wife and I keep getting into it about the mortgage, the business, all of that.
